How to Isolate AI Coding Agents With Docker Sandboxes v0.54.0 and AI Governance

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at mangodeveloper.com

Running an autonomous coding agent like Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, or Kiro directly on your host workstation gives that agent the same filesystem, network, and credential reach as your own user account. A single prompt injection buried inside an untrusted repository, dependency script, or issue thread can read ~/.ssh, exfiltrate environment variables, or invoke destructive Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools before you notice a terminal command ran.

Standard containers were built to isolate predictable application workloads, whereas AI agents act as autonomous operators that decide what code to write, which packages to pull, and which external endpoints to query. In mid-November 2026, Docker shipped Docker Sandboxes v0.54.0 alongside its Docker AI Governance architecture, wrapping each coding agent inside a dedicated microVM with enforceable network proxies, mount policies, Cedar-based MCP tool rules, and structured SIEM audit logs.

This guide walks through setting up Docker Sandboxes v0.54.0 from scratch, locking down outbound network traffic with a default-deny baseline, building reusable sandbox templates via snapshots and Dockerfiles, enforcing Cedar policies across the MCP Gateway, and migrating past the breaking SSH and Git transport changes introduced in the November 2026 releases. All commands, settings keys, and policy syntax below are drawn from the official Docker Sandboxes release notes, Docker AI Governance documentation, and Mohammad-Ali A'rabi's v0.51+ toolchain walkthrough.

A standard docker run container shares the host Linux kernel (or the shared Linux VM kernel inside Docker Desktop on macOS and Windows). If you grant an agent inside a regular container access to /var/run/docker.sock so it can build and test container images, that agent effectively gains root-equivalent control over the host Docker daemon.

Docker Sandboxes solves that architectural problem by giving each sandbox its own lightweight microVM managed by the sbx CLI and background sandboxd daemon. Every sandbox runs an isolated kernel, a private internal Docker Engine, a dedicated filesystem boundary, and a host-enforced network proxy.

When you combine Docker Sandboxes with Docker AI Governance and Docker Hardened Images (DHI), the security model splits cleanly across three enforcement layers and a verifiable supply chain:

That microVM approach contrasts with cluster-level sandboxing strategies like Google's Agent-Substrate and gVisor donation to the CNCF, giving individual developer laptops and cloud sandboxes a local-first enforcement boundary.

Docker AI Governance layers covering Sandbox Policies, Cedar MCP Tool Governance, and SIEM Audit Logs

Before installing docker-sbx, confirm your workstation meets the hardware virtualization requirements for local microVMs:

Note that the sbx CLI runs its own microVM runtime and does not require a host Docker Engine just to launch sandboxes. You only need a host Docker Engine or Docker Desktop installation if you plan to build custom sandbox templates from a Dockerfile using docker build and docker image save.

On Ubuntu 24.04 or 26.04, configure Docker's official apt repository without auto-installing the full container engine, then install the docker-sbx package and verify /dev/kvm membership:

curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sudo REPO_ONLY=1 sh sudo apt update sudo apt install -y docker-sbx sudo usermod -aG kvm "$USER"

On macOS with Apple silicon, install the CLI directly from Docker's Homebrew tap:

shell brew trust docker/tap brew install docker/tap/sbx

Once installed, check the binary version (which prints the client release without starting sandboxd) and authenticate with your Docker account:

If you are upgrading an existing installation to v0.54.0 (released November 16, 2026), execute two mandatory migration steps right away. First, refresh your generated SSH configuration so older OpenSSH clients are protected against crafted host or username command injection:

shell sbx setup ssh sbx --cloud setup ssh

Second, if you ever run sbx as root on Unix, note that v0.54.0 ignores inherited path overrides (HOME, XDG_CONFIG_HOME, XDG_STATE_HOME, XDG_CACHE_HOME, SANDBOXES_STORAGE_ROOT, and DOCKER_SANDBOXES_API) when running as root, locking root execution to root's registered home directory. Stop any older root daemon using custom storage paths before upgrading.

To enable Docker Hardened Image templates across your sandboxes, note that the legacy DOCKER_SANDBOXES_USE_DHI environment variable was retired in v0.53.0. Set the persistent configuration key instead:

plaintext sbx settings set platform.images.useDHI true

By default, an unconstrained coding agent can reach arbitrary external domains. Before spinning up a workspace, initialize a global default-deny network baseline so every outbound connection must pass an explicit allow rule.

If you have used Docker Sandboxes previously and want to clear stale rules first, run sbx policy reset (which reports how many user-added and approval-granted rules will be removed) and then initialize the deny-all preset:

plaintext sbx policy reset sbx policy init deny-all sbx policy ls

Because network policies are enforced by the host-side sandbox proxy instead of inside the guest VM, an agent cannot bypass them by editing /etc/hosts, changing iptables, or modifying environment variables. You can verify the effective policy from your host terminal before starting any agent:

plaintext sbx policy check network github.com:443 sbx policy check network api.anthropic.com

In v0.54.0, sbx policy check network names the exact rule that permitted or blocked a target and explains when an HTTP-level policy check (sbx policy check http) is required. Keep in mind that v0.53.0 updated HTTP policy matching to treat ASCII letters in URL paths and query parameter names and values case-insensitively, so audit any custom HTTP rules that previously relied on uppercase or lowercase path distinctions.

Also check sbx policy --help if you block specific hostnames on CDN-backed origins: when an allowed hostname and a denied hostname share the same underlying IP address, IP-layer connection checks can overlap unless inspected at the HTTP/TLS proxy layer.

Starting in v0.51.0, built-in agent names resolve to published Kit v3 images hosted at docker.io/sbx/NAME:latest, covering claude, codex, cursor, devin, docker-agent, opencode, kiro, copilot, droid, and shell. Note that the built-in gemini agent name was retired in v0.53.0.

You can launch a sandbox in two workspace modes:

In v0.54.0, clone-mode Git transport received a major security upgrade. Previously, fetching from the sandbox-NAME Git remote used an unauthenticated localhost TCP port (9418), which exposed the repository history to other local user accounts on multi-user hosts. Version 0.54.0 routes sandbox-NAME fetches over the Unix daemon socket restricted strictly to your host user account and blocks publishing TCP port 9418. Only enable legacy TCP transport (sbx settings set clone.legacyTcpRemote true) if you use a non-standard Git client that ignores custom uploadpack settings.

Let's create an isolated shell sandbox named sbx-workspace mounted to the current project directory, or add --clone for a private Git copy:

shell sbx create --name sbx-workspace shell . sbx run --name sbx-workspace

When you need to attach a secondary reference repository as read-only so the agent can read shared libraries without modifying them, pass PATH:TARGET:ro at creation time:

shell sbx create --name sbx-multi --workspace ../shared-lib:/workspace/shared-lib:ro shell .

Once inside sbx-workspace, check the guest operating system and its configured apt sources. Even if your host machine runs Ubuntu 26.04 or macOS, the default shell kit runs Debian 13 (Trixie):

shell cat /etc/os-release find /etc/apt -maxdepth 2 -type f ( -name '.list' -o -name '.sources' ) -exec cat {} +

In the default Debian 13 shell kit, package metadata and security updates pull from deb.debian.org, while Docker Hardened Image packages pull from dhi.io. If you run sudo apt update right now under the deny-all policy, the proxy will reject the connection (often surfacing as a 403 error followed by a misleading "repository is not signed" warning from apt).

Open a second terminal on your host and allow only the package mirrors and LLM API endpoints required for that specific sandbox:

shell sbx policy allow network --sandbox sbx-workspace 'deb.debian.org,dhi.io,api.anthropic.com' sbx policy check network --sandbox sbx-workspace deb.debian.org

Back inside the sandbox terminal, sudo apt update and package installations will now succeed cleanly while every other domain on the internet stays blocked.

Installing heavy toolchains like Node.js, Rust, Python data science stacks, or TeX Live inside a fresh sandbox every morning wastes bandwidth and disk I/O. Docker Sandboxes supports two workflows for turning a configured environment into a reusable template, mirroring the difference between docker commit and docker build.

Comparison flowchart of sbx template save snapshots and Dockerfile tar import into the microVM store

If you installed packages interactively inside sbx-workspace and want to freeze that exact root filesystem for future projects, stop the sandbox from your host terminal and save a template snapshot:

shell sbx stop sbx-workspace sbx template save sbx-workspace my-team-stack:v1 sbx template ls

Keep three security boundaries in mind when snapshotting:

To spin up a brand-new sandbox from your saved snapshot without querying a remote registry, pass --template and --pull never:

shell sbx create --name sbx-fast-clone --template my-team-stack:v1 --pull never shell . sbx run --name sbx-fast-clone

Snapshots are fast, but they do not document which package versions were installed. For version-controlled team environments, write a Dockerfile that extends Docker's official standalone base image (docker.io/sbx/shell-image:latest, or docker.io/sbx/shell-docker-image:latest if your agent needs an internal Docker daemon). Do not use the old docker/sandbox-templates:shell tag, which was retired in v0.51.0.

Note one important OS difference documented in November 2026 testing: while the interactive shell kit runs Debian 13, the standalone docker.io/sbx/shell-image:latest base image runs Ubuntu 26.04 (Resolute). Always extend an official sbx/ base image instead of a plain ubuntu:26.04 image so the mandatory agent user, init wrappers, and sandbox hooks remain intact.

docker FROM docker.io/sbx/shell-image:latest USER root RUN apt-get update && DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends nodejs npm python3-pip ripgrep jq curl && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/* USER agent

Always switch back to USER agent at the end of the Dockerfile so the coding agent does not execute as root inside the microVM.

Because Docker Sandboxes maintains its own microVM image store completely separate from your host Docker Engine, running docker build alone does not make the image visible to sbx. Export the built image to a .tar archive and import it into the sandbox image store with sbx template load:

shell docker build -t my-team-stack:v2 ./sbx-template docker image save my-team-stack:v2 -o my-team-stack-v2.tar sbx template load my-team-stack-v2.tar sbx create --name sbx-declarative --template my-team-stack:v2 --pull never shell .

To monitor how much disk space your accumulated templates, containers, and volumes consume, run the disk usage command introduced in v0.53.0:

shell sbx df --verbose sbx template ls --all --digests --no-trunc

Once an AI agent is running inside its microVM, you need controlled ways to expose local dev servers, inject API credentials without leaking plaintext strings, and govern which MCP tools the agent is allowed to invoke.

Version 0.54.0 deprecated the legacy sbx ports command in favor of structured sbx port subcommands that work identically across local and cloud sandboxes:

shell sbx port publish sbx-declarative 3000:3000 sbx port ls sbx-declarative sbx port unpublish sbx-declarative 3000:3000

If you omit the host port (sbx port publish sbx-declarative :3000), Docker Sandboxes assigns an available loopback port automatically. Starting in v0.53.0, unpublishing an automatically assigned port keeps it unpublished across sandbox restarts (fixing CVE-2026-126330).

Never pass raw API keys on the command line where they persist in shell history or ps output. Use --value-stdin with an explicit destination host so the sandbox proxy substitutes the credential only on requests to that exact domain:

shell printf '%s' "$ANTHROPIC_API_KEY" | sbx secret set-custom anthropic_key --value-stdin --host api.anthropic.com

Starting in v0.51.0, custom secrets without an explicit destination host no longer substitute credentials, preventing accidental token leakage to untrusted endpoints.

When agents connect to external systems through the Model Context Protocol, making an MCP server available should not grant blanket permission to run destructive tools. Docker AI Governance routes MCP calls through the Docker MCP Gateway and evaluates organization rules written in the Cedar policy language.

To inspect which MCP mode (static or dynamic) and servers are attached to a sandbox versus registered on your host, run:

shell sbx inspect sbx-declarative sbx mcp ls

You can also prefix MCP tool names with their server identifier to prevent naming collisions across multiple servers:

plaintext sbx settings set mcp.useServerNamePrefixes true

In Docker AI Governance, MCP requests are denied by default when policy enforcement is active unless a Cedar permit rule matches. For example, to allow agents to invoke only read-only tools on an internal MCP server named internal-tracker, define a Cedar policy checking the tool's readOnly annotation:

rego permit (principal, action == MCP::Action::"invokeTool", resource) when { resource in MCP::Server::"internal-tracker" && resource.readOnly == true };

As GitLab 19.6's new MCP governance controls also highlight, tool annotations like readOnly are supplied by the MCP server metadata, so combine Cedar tool policies with network-level restrictions. Keep one architectural boundary clear: Docker's Cedar MCP policies apply to traffic routed through the Docker MCP Gateway. If an agent inside a sandbox connects directly to a remote HTTP MCP server on its own, that connection is governed by the sandbox's outbound network policy instead.

Diagram of a microVM coding agent connecting through a Cedar MCP policy gate and Unix Git socket

If your team already knows standard Docker commands, the table below maps everyday container workflows to their sbx microVM equivalents alongside common v0.54.0 troubleshooting fixes.

Two operational issues frequently trip up enterprise rollouts on corporate networks or Linux workstations:

Docker Sandboxes enables post-quantum TLS key exchange by default (tls.postQuantumKeyExchange = true). Some enterprise TLS-inspecting firewalls and proxies (including older Zscaler, Palo Alto Networks, and F5 configurations) drop or stall handshakes that advertise post-quantum key exchange or ML-DSA signatures. If sbx login or kit pulls hang behind a corporate proxy, disable post-quantum key exchange in v0.54.0 and restart the daemon:

shell sbx settings set tls.postQuantumKeyExchange false sbx daemon restart

Similarly, if your laptop frequently switches between office Wi-Fi and split-tunnel VPNs, Docker Sandboxes automatically closes stalled outbound connections after 30 seconds so agents can reconnect cleanly. You can tune that threshold with sbx settings set egress.stalledConnectionTimeoutSeconds 15 followed by sbx daemon restart.

When automating sandbox checks in CI or troubleshooting a reluctant local daemon, use structured JSON output from sbx diagnose (introduced in v0.54.0) and match on the stable id field instead of the display name:

shell sbx diagnose --format json | jq '.checks[] | select(.status != "pass") | {id, status, message}'

If sandboxd ever stops responding on Linux or macOS, you can dump live goroutine stacks without killing active agent sessions by sending SIGUSR1 to the daemon (kill -USR1 $(pidof sandboxd)), or run sbx diagnose --upload to capture rotated crash logs with usernames and home-directory paths automatically redacted.

Finally, once your local kit and network policies are tested, you can move workloads to Docker-managed cloud sandboxes or schedule recurring background tasks without leaving your laptop online:

shell sbx --cloud schedule create --name nightly-audit --workdir /workspace --every 24h sbx/claude -- "run security audit" sbx --cloud schedule ls

Combining --clone mode on local repositories, a deny-all outbound proxy baseline, Cedar MCP gateway rules, and version-controlled sbx/shell-image templates gives coding agents the freedom to build, test, and refactor inside a microVM while keeping your host credentials and production networks out of reach.

Efficient GDAL Workflows: Reading Zipped Archives In-Place

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at u11d.com

Geodata rarely arrives as a single file. A Shapefile is at least three files (.shp, .shx, .dbf) and usually a .prj, so users, survey contractors, and public data portals zip them. Your upload endpoint receives a .zip, hands it to GDAL, and gets this:

That is GDAL 3.13. Earlier versions print only the first sentence, without the hint, and before 3.10 it reads "not recognized as a supported file format". Called from code, the same failure can return None with an empty error message. Even with the fix GDAL suggests, some valid archives still refuse to open, and a path that works on your laptop fails once the service runs from a different working directory.

The short answer: prefix the path with /vsizip/, as in /vsizip/roads.zip. GDAL then reads the archive in place, from disk, memory, or object storage, without extracting anything. The rest of this guide covers doing that safely in an upload handler, using the Python bindings: reading uploads from disk or memory, validating what is inside, the archive layouts that still fail, and how to get a useful error when they do.

GDAL drivers read files; they do not unpack archives. /vsizip/ is a virtual file system, not a driver: prefix a path with it and GDAL presents the contents of the zip as a directory. The driver that opens the data (Shapefile, GeoJSON, GeoPackage) never knows an archive was involved.

That design has three consequences that explain almost every surprise in the rest of this guide:

With it, the Shapefile driver reads the bundle inside:

The same path works anywhere GDAL accepts a data source, including ogr2ogr. This converts one layer of a zipped upload straight to GeoPackage:

If you control the file name, there is a shortcut. Since GDAL 3.1, the Shapefile driver opens an archive named *.shp.zip (one or several layers) or *.shz (a single layer) directly, with no prefix:

This only helps for Shapefiles, and only when the extension is exactly .shp.zip or .shz. For uploads, where the user picks the name, use /vsizip/ explicitly.

If the upload already has a path, use it. A file saved by an earlier step, an object in S3 (Step 6), or a Django upload larger than FILE_UPLOAD_MAX_MEMORY_SIZE (2.5 MB by default), which arrives as a TemporaryUploadedFile with temporary_file_path(), can go straight to /vsizip/. GDAL then reads only the parts of the archive it needs, where the file already is. Reading the file into Python first, only to hand GDAL a copy, loads the whole archive into memory for nothing.

Some uploads have no path. Smaller Django uploads stay in memory as an InMemoryUploadedFile; Flask and FastAPI keep small uploads in memory and spool larger ones to anonymous temporary files, and give you a file object either way; a message queue or an HTTP client gives you bytes. For those, /vsimem/ is GDAL's in-memory file system: register the bytes under a path, then chain /vsizip/ on top of it.

The double slash in /vsizip//vsimem/… is not a typo: it is /vsizip/ followed by the absolute path /vsimem/…. The same holds for a file on disk: /vsizip/ followed by /srv/uploads/roads.zip is /vsizip//srv/uploads/roads.zip.

Before handing an upload to a driver, look at what is inside. gdal.ReadDirRecursive lists an archive's members through the same virtual file system, without a driver:

On the command line, gdal vsi list (GDAL 3.11 and later; 3.11 prints the list as JSON) does the same:

The member list lets you reject an archive that holds no vector data with a message that says what it does hold, instead of a generic "unsupported format", and it tells you which members to try when opening the archive as a whole fails.

What /vsizip/upload.zip gives you depends on what is inside it. With several Shapefiles, each becomes a layer of one dataset:

With several GeoJSON files, the same path fails, because the GeoJSON driver does not accept a directory:

Whether a single file needs its member named depends on what else is in the zip. roads_gml.zip holds the .gml and its .xsd, so the archive is a directory: /vsizip/roads_gml.zip fails and /vsizip/roads_gml.zip/roads.gml opens. one_geojson.zip holds nothing but roads.geojson, so GDAL resolves the archive path to that one member, and the layer takes its name from the zip rather than from the file:

Naming the member works in every single-file case and keeps the layer name predictable, so that is the path to prefer when the archive holds one vector file.

So an upload handler needs a short, ordered list of paths to try. The function below puts Steps 2–4 together. open_zip takes any /vsizip/ path, whether the archive is on disk, in memory, or in object storage. It lists the members, rejects archives with no vector data, tries the member named after the requested layer, then the only vector member if there is just one, then the archive as a whole. open_zip_bytes wraps it for uploads that exist only as bytes and frees the in-memory buffer whatever happens:

UploadRejected covers problems with the upload itself and maps to a 4xx response with its message. A missing file or missing storage credentials is not the user's fault, so GDAL's own RuntimeError is left to propagate. When the upload has a path, call open_zip("/vsizip/" + os.path.abspath(path)); when it is bytes or a file object, use with open_zip_bytes(upload.read()) as (ds, names): and finish with the dataset inside the block. To try it from the command line, save the code as read_upload.py with this wrapper at the bottom. The wrapper has the file's path, so it passes the path, not the file's bytes:

Run against the sample archives, it gives:

Every rejection names what the archive actually contains, so the user can fix the upload without a support ticket. Feeding the same archives through open_zip_bytes gives the same results, and afterwards gdal.ReadDir("/vsimem/") returns None: no buffers are left behind, including on the rejection paths.

How much GDAL tells you about a failed open depends on how you call it.

With exceptions enabled, as in every example above, the Python bindings raise a RuntimeError that carries GDAL's full message:

Without gdal.UseExceptions(), the same call returns None, and the last error message is empty:

GDAL 3.8 and later also print a FutureWarning here (3.6 does not): "Neither gdal.UseExceptions() nor gdal.DontUseExceptions() has been explicitly called. In GDAL 4.0, exceptions will be enabled by default." Call gdal.UseExceptions() once at import time and the empty-message case disappears.

The reason is a flag on the underlying C call. GDALOpenEx only records the "not recognized" message when it is passed GDAL_OF_VERBOSE_ERROR. Opening the same archive from C, once without and once with the flag, shows the difference:

If you call GDAL from C or C++, or through another language binding, and failed opens come back with an empty message, check whether the open passes GDAL_OF_VERBOSE_ERROR (in Python, gdal.OF_VERBOSE_ERROR).

Because virtual file systems chain, a zip does not have to be local or even in memory. A common upload design has the client send the file straight to an S3 bucket with a presigned URL, and a worker process it afterwards. With /vsis3/ under /vsizip/, the worker reads the archive where it lies, without downloading it first.

Opening and listing work the same way as for a local archive:

open_zip from Step 4 takes any /vsizip/ path, so the worker validates the object in the bucket with the same function and nothing else changes:

GDAL picks up AWS credentials the usual way, from environment variables or ~/.aws/credentials. If none are found, it says exactly what is missing, and open_zip lets that error through rather than blaming the upload:

For a public bucket, set AWS_NO_SIGN_REQUEST=YES (or gdal.SetConfigOption("AWS_NO_SIGN_REQUEST", "YES")) to read without credentials. For S3-compatible storage outside AWS, such as the self-hosted options in our MinIO alternatives comparison, point /vsis3/ at it with the AWS_S3_ENDPOINT configuration option (host and port, such as minio.internal:9000). Self-hosted servers usually also need AWS_VIRTUAL_HOSTING=FALSE for path-style bucket URLs, and AWS_HTTPS=NO if they serve plain HTTP. With only the endpoint set, GDAL requests https://./… and the open fails.

The same chaining works over plain HTTP with /vsicurl/, which reads a file using range requests:

The path after /vsizip/ is resolved against the process's working directory, not against anything else. The same command from a different directory fails:

Services, task workers, and desktop apps launched from a file manager rarely run from the directory you expect. Convert to an absolute path (os.path.abspath) before adding the prefix.

The zip holds more than one member, so GDAL presents it as a directory, and the driver that matches the file inside does not accept one. GeoJSON and GML need the member named explicitly (/vsizip/upload.zip/file.geojson) whenever anything else is in the zip, even just an .xsd schema or a readme. List the members with gdal.ReadDirRecursive and open the member path.

Finder's "Compress" adds a __MACOSX/ folder with a ._ metadata file for each file in the archive, so roads.geojson comes with __MACOSX/._roads.geojson. A member filter that only checks extensions counts both as vector data. Skip members under __MACOSX/ and names starting with ._, as _is_vector in Step 4 does.

With missing credentials GDAL says so explicitly, but with wrong credentials, a key that does not exist, or no permission on the bucket, the exception only says the path does not exist in the file system. The HTTP status appears separately, as a warning such as HTTP response code on http://…/roads.zip: 403. Log GDAL's warnings (for example with gdal.PushErrorHandler) in the worker, so a 403 is not mistaken for a missing upload.

Exceptions are not enabled, and the open was not passed the verbose-error flag. Call gdal.UseExceptions() at import time; in C or another binding, add GDAL_OF_VERBOSE_ERROR to the open flags.

Every request writes to the same /vsimem/ path, so a second upload overwrites the first while it is still being read. /vsimem/ is shared across the whole process; generate a unique name per request (for example with uuid.uuid4()).

/vsimem/ files are never freed automatically. Call gdal.Unlink() on the in-memory path in a finally block, so rejected uploads are freed too. If the upload is already on disk, skip /vsimem/ entirely and open it by path.

Zipped geodata does not need to be extracted to be read. /vsizip/ exposes the archive as a directory wherever it is stored (on disk, in S3, or in /vsimem/ when the upload exists only as bytes), so one handler can inspect, validate, and read a Shapefile bundle without extracting it. The remaining work is in the edge cases: naming members for drivers that do not read directories, resolving paths absolutely, and making sure failures carry a message your users can act on.

Prefix the path with /vsizip/, for example ogrinfo /vsizip/roads.zip or gdal.OpenEx("/vsizip/roads.zip", gdal.OF_VECTOR) in Python. GDAL's /vsizip/ virtual file system presents the zip as a directory, and the Shapefile driver reads the .shp, .shx, and .dbf inside it in place, without writing anything to disk.

GDAL drivers do not unpack archives, so a bare .zip path is treated as a data file that no driver can read. Add the /vsizip/ prefix. If the error persists with the prefix, the zip holds several files and the one you want is a format such as GeoJSON or GML whose driver cannot open a directory; name the member explicitly, as in /vsizip/upload.zip/rivers.geojson.

Write the bytes to GDAL's in-memory file system with gdal.FileFromMemBuffer("/vsimem/.zip", data), then open /vsizip//vsimem/.zip with gdal.OpenEx. Use a unique name per request, because /vsimem/ is shared across the process, and call gdal.Unlink() on the path after closing the dataset to free the memory.

Call gdal.ReadDirRecursive("/vsizip/upload.zip") in Python, or run gdal vsi list /vsizip/upload.zip on the command line. Both list the archive's members through GDAL's /vsizip/ virtual file system without opening any driver, so an upload handler can reject an archive with no .shp, .gpkg, or .geojson inside, or decide which member to open, before reading any data.

GDAL presents a zip with more than one member as a directory, and the GeoJSON driver does not accept a directory. Only a zip whose sole member is the GeoJSON file opens through the bare archive path. List the archive's members with gdal.ReadDirRecursive("/vsizip/upload.zip") and open the GeoJSON member by its full path, such as /vsizip/upload.zip/data.geojson. The same applies to GML, where the .xsd schema alone is enough to make the zip a directory.

GDAL's Python bindings are running without exceptions enabled, and GDALOpenEx only records the reason for a failed open when the verbose-error flag is set. Call gdal.UseExceptions() once at import time, and failed opens raise a RuntimeError with GDAL's full message. In C or other bindings, pass GDAL_OF_VERBOSE_ERROR in the open flags.

A relative path after /vsizip/ is resolved against the process's current working directory, which is usually different in a deployed service or worker. Convert the file path to an absolute path before adding the prefix, for example "/vsizip/" + os.path.abspath(path).

Yes. GDAL virtual file systems can be chained: /vsizip//vsicurl/https://example.com/data.zip reads a zip over HTTP using range requests, and /vsizip//vsis3/bucket/key.zip reads one from Amazon S3 using the standard AWS credentials (AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY, or ~/.aws/credentials). For a public bucket, set the AWS_NO_SIGN_REQUEST configuration option to YES to read without credentials.

Opaque Sessions Versus JWTs for Revocable Web App Access After Phone Login

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A successful phone code should not buy an abusive shopper access that the store cannot withdraw. For a web app choosing an opaque session versus a JWT, revocation is the operational constraint that determines my default when adding phone one-time-code login.

Short answer: use opaque sessions for the browser when incident response requires immediate revocation, and budget for a session lookup on authenticated requests. JWTs allow offline verification, but a valid token ordinarily remains usable until expiry unless the verifier consults additional revocation state. Sessions at the edge and short-lived JWTs internally can be a reasonable compromise.

Consider a production-response rehearsal, not a report of an actual incident: I am reviewing the launch plan, phone verification succeeds, and an automated client begins using its new session to reach account and checkout functions. The responder revokes that session. The next authorization decision is the test. If a verifier accepts the old credential because its signature and expiration still check out, the phone login worked while containment failed.

The invariant is narrower than preventing every bot: after the authoritative session record is revoked, subsequent authorization checks must reject it. Requests already authorized are a separate boundary. An application shouldn't promise that deleting a session rolls back a purchase that has already committed.

Phone verification establishes that someone could complete that challenge; it doesn't settle whether the resulting activity should continue. For this rollout, I would review code-request abuse, verification attempts, and authenticated activity as separate control points, because stopping a session doesn't stop a client from requesting another code, while throttling code delivery doesn't invalidate an existing credential. The session decision belongs inside that larger abuse plan.

An opaque identifier makes the server's record authoritative.

The credential alone doesn't tell the application whether access is still allowed, so the application asks the session store, checks expiration and revocation, and only then proceeds. That lookup is the mechanism behind immediate revocation, not incidental overhead to optimize away during the first traffic spike.

A JWT shifts part of that decision into the credential. A verifier can validate its signature and claims without asking the issuer about each request. The operational catch is straightforward: a signature proves that a token was issued, not that the issuer still wants it accepted. Expiration bounds that exposure; it doesn't make the exposure disappear.

A revocation list or a current-account-state lookup can change the result. It also changes the capacity plan, because a supposedly offline path now depends on shared state.

Revoking a refresh token shouldn't be described as instantly invalidating every already-issued access token. Those are different credentials with different acceptance rules.

For a planning example, assume 400 authenticated requests per second during normal shopping and a threefold promotion surge. With one session lookup per request, the surge requires 1,200 lookups per second before retries, operational headroom, or any additional account checks. These are illustrative workload assumptions, not measured throughput or a vendor benchmark. Replace them with the existing application's traffic distribution before choosing storage capacity.

The phone-code send rate is a poor proxy for session-store demand: one successful login can lead to many authenticated requests, and abusive traffic can keep presenting a credential long after the login flow ends. The store therefore needs its own latency budget and saturation signals. An authentication SLO should count a legitimate shopper's ability to complete an authorized request, while a separate containment objective tracks how quickly revocation reaches every verifier. Combining those into one success percentage can conceal a security failure behind good availability.

Caching deserves particular skepticism. If a verifier caches an accepted session for 30 seconds without checking invalidation, that deployment has accepted a potential 30-second revocation delay. Calling the underlying record immediately revocable doesn't repair the application-level promise. Either preserve authoritative checks, establish an invalidation mechanism with a defensible bound, or document the weaker containment objective.

The dependency also has a failure policy. For protected operations, a lookup that cannot establish validity should deny access; treating an unavailable store as permission to proceed defeats the reason for selecting opaque sessions. That policy can affect availability during a storage incident. Budget and test for it.

A product's ability to issue credentials is only the beginning of the comparison. The review question is what the existing app will consult before allowing an abusive account to act again, and who owns that dependency during an incident. The following are selection boundaries, not claims that any product automatically satisfies a particular SLO.

Infrai offers one key, one bill and one REST API for 295 routes across 20 modules: a broad capability surface with a simple consistent interface. Adding a capability means another endpoint under the same contract, with no SDK to install and no separate API keys or invoices to reconcile. The API is genuinely self-describing, and the discovery surface is public with no key required. Every documented capability ships runnable examples in 10 languages, which helps a platform team inspect a Go integration before adopting it; the session integration still needs to satisfy the same application acceptance test.

The trade-off is an external dependency. For a team whose requirement is fully self-hosted identity, choose Keycloak or application-owned sessions instead; breadth behind a consistent interface does not resolve an operational-control requirement.

The buy-versus-build decision should include who gets paged. A managed service reduces the infrastructure the platform team operates but introduces an external dependency and a contract that application code must follow. Self-hosting gives the team more operational control while making recovery and upgrades its responsibility. Neither arrangement removes the need to prove that every protected path respects revoked access.

The preventative path starts with verification rather than trusting possession of a credential. This runnable Go probe calls the session-verification operation with an existing session identifier. Set INFRAI_API_ORIGIN to the service's HTTPS API origin, without a path, INFRAI_API_KEY to a server-side API key, and SESSION_ID to the identifier being checked. Keep these values out of browser code.

This is a transport probe, not an authorization middleware: a successful HTTP exchange still requires interpreting the documented response schema before granting access. It prints the response for inspection, so run it in a controlled environment rather than forwarding authentication data into production logs.

The request is read-only, so retrying it does not create a second session. The bounded retry handles HTTP 429 with exponential backoff and respects a longer Retry-After delay. A user-facing request would also need a total deadline aligned with the application's SLO; this diagnostic can wait longer than a checkout should. Save it as main.go and run it with Go after setting the three environment variables.

An HTTP success is not an authorization decision.

Before launch, exercise the application boundary: establish a session after a successful phone challenge, confirm that the protected operation accepts it, revoke it, then repeat that operation with the same credential. The second operation must be rejected. Repeat across application instances and any cache boundary; checking the administrative record alone does not prove that the checkout path consults it. Keep this check separate from code-delivery tests, since issuing another phone code and honoring an existing session create different abuse opportunities.

The opaque-session default does not apply when a verifier genuinely cannot call back and can tolerate access remaining valid until expiry. JWTs fit that constraint. The decision should name the accepted exposure window and the service that owns it, rather than treating statelessness as a universal performance requirement.

Sessions at the browser edge with JWTs internally are a legitimate middle ground, provided the internal token's remaining lifetime is included in the containment promise. Revoking the browser session prevents subsequent issuance through that session; it does not retract an internal credential already issued to a verifier that checks only its signature and expiry.

For this store, the launch decision is therefore concrete: budget the authoritative lookup, reject credentials whose validity cannot be established, and prove that revoked access stops at every protected entry point. The phone code gets the shopper in. The session policy determines whether the responder can close the door.

References for authentication controls, token semantics, and the product boundaries compared above:

Zero-upload B2B workflows with Canvas, Web Crypto, and plain JavaScript

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A sample closeout PDF has a before photo for work-order line WO-02 and an empty after slot. The companion CSV flags that same line for review. A sample wholesale order form has SKU CEDAR-8 at $12.00 for boutiques and $10.20 for distributors. The buyer gets only their group's price.

Both jobs start with a local file picker. The browser turns the input into records, runs checks on those records, and produces a file the operator can inspect before sending. Canvas transforms, Web Crypto digests, GS1 check digits, and CSV quoting can all run without npm packages. The production workbenches use React for the UI and pdf-lib for direct PDF downloads. Both request an export permit using project IDs, counts, and export metadata, then render the file locally from the draft.

CloseoutPack attaches the before and after photos to a numbered line with a scope and status. Its JPEG normalizer reads EXIF orientation and capture time, removes the EXIF segments from the bytes passed to the decoder, and applies the orientation once on Canvas. That prevents a browser that honors EXIF from rotating the same image a second time. The workbench caps the rendered long edge at 1,400 pixels, encodes a JPEG, and closes the decoded bitmap. The capture time travels as a separate field for the audit. The picker accepts JPEG, PNG, and WebP, so an iPhone HEIC original needs a supported copy.

The audit compares the timestamps when both are parseable. If Before is later than After, it flags a possible swap for review. Missing timestamps leave the order to the operator. The same line number anchors the photo pair in the US Letter PDF and the findings in the open-items CSV. CloseoutPack · WHATWG Canvas specification

The browser-only example below lets the decoder apply embedded orientation. Serve it on HTTPS or localhost. Pass a local image File to localJpeg. Hash the input for an exact-byte repeat check, or hash the generated JPEG to identify that exact export. Run await sha256Test() to check the digest against a known value.

SHA-256 is a reference pattern here for a local duplicate check or export manifest. The CloseoutPack audit uses line IDs, photo presence, status, and timestamps. Two encodes of the same scene can produce different bytes. A digest identifies bytes and provides no evidence of when or where the photo was taken. Decode and hash one photo at a time on mobile. A 4,000 × 3,000 RGBA buffer alone is 48,000,000 bytes, and Web Crypto's digest() takes the full input in memory. Web Cryptography API

The paperwork around the report has its own rounding and filtering rules.

For a standalone photo appendix, seven photos at four per page use two Letter pages, split 4 + 3. The photo-sheet tool prints through the browser's Save as PDF dialog. Photo Sheet Builder

LineSheetPack reads the Shopify or ordinary CSV with File.text(). Its parser handles commas and line breaks inside quoted cells, then maps modern or legacy headers into typed catalog rows. Keep a Shopify product export as the source and identify each sellable variant by SKU in the buyer catalog. An image-only continuation row belongs to the product's media. Counting it as a variant inflates the assortment. Shopify product CSV reference · RFC 4180

For each buyer group, select its assortment, resolve the per-SKU override or the group's discount, then render the PDF and order CSV from that same selection. The order CSV leaves Order Qty blank for the buyer. Without an override, calculate the discounted unit price once in integer cents.

Group A at zero discount stays at $12.00. Group B at 15% uses $10.20. The $1.01 half-price case lands at exactly 50.5 cents and rounds up once. The production code uses bounded safe integers for this calculation. The reference snippet uses BigInt.

An MOQ of 25 with six units per case takes five cases, or 30 units, in MOQ Calc. LineSheetPack flags 25 as incompatible with the pack and expects a corrected minimum such as 30 before export. At a $6.00 landed cost, the $12.00 wholesale offer earns $6.00 gross per unit and $180.00 on 30 units. MOQ Calc · LineSheetPack

Store a GTIN as a string. Starting at the rightmost payload digit, multiply by 3, 1, 3, 1 and continue left. For UPC payload 03600029145, the weighted sum is 58. The check digit is (10 - (58 % 10)) % 10 = 2, yielding 036000291452. After validation, a 14-digit padded key lets a batch detect that 00036000291452 represents the same identifier. The checksum checks composition. Assignment and ownership come from GS1 records. GS1 General Specifications · Barcode Lab GTIN checker

CSV quoting protects the structure of a row. A spreadsheet can still evaluate a quoted cell as a formula after parsing it. LineSheetPack checks the first relevant character of untrusted text, prefixes formula-like values with an apostrophe, and then doubles embedded quotes and wraps the cell. Validated price and quantity fields take a separate numeric path.

Apply that guard at the export boundary. Keep the source values for review and preserve leading zeroes by importing identifier columns into spreadsheets as text. CSV Clean's variant validator labels an apostrophe-modified file -audit.csv for spreadsheet review. Fix flagged values in the source and regenerate the Shopify import copy. Spreadsheet behavior varies, especially after saving and reopening a CSV. Exercise the guard in the receiving application with a fixture that includes whitespace, quotes, and a harmless formula. OWASP CSV Injection · CSV Clean variant validator

At fulfillment, carry the buyer's PO and SKUs into the packing slip. If one SKU has 24 ordered and 18 packed, and another has 12 ordered and 14 packed, report six short and two extra. Netting those counts would hide two distinct corrections. Pack Slip

Disclosure. This teardown covers tools from our own portfolio. AI assisted the writing.

Receipt Email Provider Comparison: API Domain Verification and Suppression Events

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Choose a receipt email provider by the effort needed to connect a settled payment to a branded, observable message. For a media subscription app, the evaluation constraint is straightforward: a payment notification must not become permission to send until settlement is confirmed, and replaying that notification must not create another receipt. Domain verification, suppression handling, and event history belong in the first useful test.

Short answer: compare SendGrid, Resend, and Postmark against an API-first alternative using the same receipt fixture. Try Infrai for the transactional email boundary of a beginner SaaS serving US/EU readers when a stable capability contract matters more than SMTP compatibility or pushed delivery events. Its primary attraction is that changing the vendor behind a capability preserves the application-facing contract. Public, self-describing discovery is the supporting advantage: inspect request and response schemas before adding credentials to a notebook or another SDK to production.

The trade-off is explicit: Infrai does not support SMTP relay or webhook event push. This limitation matters if receipts drive immediate downstream automation. Choose SendGrid for evaluation when its Event Webhook matches that requirement, or Postmark when an SMTP integration is the deciding constraint. No provider choice here establishes inbox placement by itself.

A successful send response is too small a result.

The useful experiment starts with a media reader buying a subscription, records the payment as settled, produces the intended receipt once, and leaves enough information to investigate the message later. Authentication and suppression controls then become acceptance criteria rather than cleanup tasks. DKIM has a defined role in signing email; RFC 6376 describes that mechanism. Treat domain authentication as a setup requirement, and evaluate delivery evidence separately.

The tempting first version is a notebook cell that sends whenever a payment notification arrives. Its weakness is the trigger: the notification and settlement are different application states, and a repeated notification can replay the side effect. The chosen design puts a deterministic receipt decision ahead of the provider adapter. This is a design comparison, not a report of a production incident or a measured benchmark.

For an AI app builder, that boundary is especially useful. A RAG feature or agent can sit elsewhere in the media product without making receipt eligibility a prompt decision. A fixed receipt template also avoids spending model tokens on content whose essential job is to reflect an order accurately. Evaluate the surrounding AI feature with its own harness; evaluate the receipt path with payment states and repeat deliveries. Mixing those tests makes a provider comparison harder to interpret.

The setup comparison needs four columns in an engineering notebook: domain authentication, credentials, client code, and the evidence returned after sending. Time to a first useful result means completing that chain, not timing the first HTTP request. No authenticated latency or setup-time benchmark is claimed here.

SendGrid is a direct-provider candidate with an email API and a documented Event Webhook surface. Its integration question is how much of that event model the receipt worker needs to understand. A team that wants pushed events should examine that contract early, including how incoming events will connect to its order records. The extra receiving integration is work, but it can serve a real operating requirement.

Resend is another direct API candidate. Start its evaluation with the sending and domain documentation, then compare its webhook contract with the receipt investigation workflow. The relevant difference from an aggregator is ownership of the provider-specific adapter: the application team chooses how much of that surface to expose internally. A small direct integration can be a sensible choice when replacing the provider is not a current requirement.

Postmark is a direct transactional-email candidate with documented API, webhook, and SMTP integration paths. That gives an existing SMTP-based application a concrete compatibility path to investigate. For this new HTTPS receipt worker, test the API path instead, and decide whether the specialist's message and event model is useful enough to adopt directly.

Infrai offers direct sending, domain authentication, event history, and suppression controls behind its capability interface. The integration benefit is a stable application contract when the backing vendor changes; that does not promise that switching from an arbitrary existing provider requires zero migration work. Its public discovery supplies full schemas and runnable examples, which helps keep the notebook-to-prod handoff grounded in an inspectable contract. Pulled events also mean the receipt worker must budget for reading event history when investigating delivery. If immediate event-driven automation is mandatory, prefer a specialist whose push contract meets that requirement.

These are different integration commitments. Infrai uses one key across backend capabilities and a single REST API, with no SDK required for native HTTP calls. That reduces credential configuration and dependency work when the app needs several backend capabilities. Count the credentials the application actually needs, the SDK or HTTP surface it adopts, and the event processing it must own. Consolidation doesn't remove sender authentication or the application's responsibility for its order records.

Start with a read-only contract inspection. This Python example fetches the discovery document for email event history, the part of the workflow needed to investigate a media subscription receipt. It sends no email and makes no delivery claim. Set INFRAI_API_KEY in the environment before running it; discovery is public and requires no key, but this example also demonstrates the authenticated request convention used by the adapter.

The returned document describes the contract; it isn't a receipt-delivery test. Build the receipt fixture separately with a synthetic order, media-172, and the state sequence pending, settled, settled. Expect zero receipt intents before settlement and one logical receipt afterward. Persist that intent with a uniqueness constraint and track its dispatch state; a worker crash between recording intent and sending must leave recoverable work. An in-memory set disappears on restart and cannot coordinate concurrent workers. Retain the same logical receipt key across retries. Application-level deduplication and provider-level idempotency solve related parts of the problem.

For Infrai, inspect the selected capability's discovery schema and idempotency declaration before writing the adapter. The platform documents the Idempotency-Key convention and a default 24-hour deduplication window; retain application order history beyond that window as your business rules require. Use the exact documented send fields rather than guessing them from a different provider's SDK. The adapter should surface response errors and back off on rate limits, honoring Retry-After when present.

The next experiment is intentionally narrower than a deliverability ranking. Run the same synthetic receipt through each shortlisted integration, with a verified sending domain and an authorized test recipient. Record the steps needed to reach that result, the credentials introduced, and the code needed to connect the resulting message record to the original order. Then test suppression handling and the investigation path. A provider with a short send example can still require substantial event plumbing.

For a pulled-event integration, measure how long your chosen polling interval takes to expose the evidence your support workflow needs. That is an application setting to evaluate, not a claim about provider latency. For a pushed-event integration, evaluate the receiving service and replay handling too. Keep both sides of the comparison honest.

Finally, ask whether the receipt worker needs a direct specialist's interfaces or a capability contract shared with the rest of the app. Choose a direct provider when its specific event or SMTP integration removes more work than it adds. Choose the capability boundary when backend consolidation and future backing-vendor changes justify adopting it now. Either choice still needs an eval-driven release gate: settlement eligibility, duplicate handling, authenticated domain setup, suppression behavior, and traceable message history.

If this capability boundary fits your receipt worker, start with the first-party transactional email integration guide and evaluate the domain, suppression, and event workflow against your own fixture.

We're a place where coders share, stay up-to-date and grow their careers.

An archaeological take on ancient rituals in the heart of a legendary Berlin nightclub

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, A Shroud Woven of Solar Threads, 2026. Installation view at Halle am Berghain Courtesy of HOUSE in partnership with Autotelic Foundation. Photo: Noshe

Press any key to continue is a monthly column about the ways technology is reshaping contemporary art, from non-fungible tokens and artificial intelligence to attention economies and new systems of distribution. Its author, Sarp Kerem Yavuz, is a visual artist, researcher and the artistic director of the Contemporary Istanbul Foundation.

“The world is changed. I feel it in the water. I feel it in the earth. I smell it in the air. Much that once was is lost, for none now live who remember it.” Cate Blanchett, as the high elf Galadriel, spoke these opening lines in The Fellowship of the Ring (2001), and in darkened cinemas around the world, her delivery set the tone for the wonder that followed. Not since my first viewing of that film 25 years ago had I felt that sense of being dwarfed by mythology, and to re-experience it in Berlin’s famous nightclub Berghain of all places was a delight.

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, an artist duo hailing from Iran and Canada, respectively, expertly blended archaeology, artificial intelligence (AI), acoustics, chemistry and sculpture to create a profoundly multi-sensory experience in A Shroud of Woven Solar Threads earlier this autumn (2-18 October).

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, Sūryasya Śántau (Within the Solar Silence), 2026. Halle am Berghain Courtesy of HOUSE in partnership with Autotelic Foundation. Photo: Noshe

Situated inside Halle am Berghain—a gargantuan former power station that, yes, was reminiscent of J.R.R. Tolkien’s Khazad-dûm—the installation was presented by the Berlin-based contemporary art initiative House, helmed by Juliet Kothe, who co-curated with Nadim Samman. The exhibition was co-presented by the Autotelic Foundation, whose whimsical take on AI art in Venice had been one of the standout digital presentations during the most recent Biennale.

At the centre of A Shroud of Woven Solar Threads was a towering, eight-minute-long, 4K video installation that slowly panned through a digitally rendered, 4,000-year-old Mithraic cave temple, created based on 3D scans of the ancient site. Originally made for an exhibition in Lausanne in 2025, the installation was expanded to take advantage of the Halle am Berghain’s size.

As the “camera” floated through the ancient temple in the looping video, visitors were encouraged to position themselves in the center of the installation, where they would be encircled by human-height black monolithic speakers. Each speaker featured a different voice, all chanting in an AI-generated, speculative Proto-Indo-Iranian (PII). PII is the ancestor of the Indo-Aryan and Iranian language families, including Sanskrit and Avestan. There are no historic documents or written texts in PII, let alone documented Mithraic chants from that period.

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, A Shroud Woven of Solar Threads, 2026. Installation view, Halle am Berghain Courtesy of HOUSE in partnership with Autotelic Foundation. Photo: Noshe

To realise the vital sonic component of the installation, the artists worked with Miguel Andrés-Toledo, a University of Toronto professor specialised in Zoroastrian languages and literature. Together they drew on comparative linguistics to reconstruct plausible linguistic forms, and AI allowed them to bring the speculative language to life. One of the speakers, Roushan said, was based on her own mother’s voice, which had been used to train one of the AI chanters.

"Mithra…" the chant began, referring to the ancient solar deity, a kind of lesser-known Ra, and as the incense burning in the hall filled one’s nostrils it was difficult not to feel transported by the sheer scale and the acoustic prowess of the installation. Near the ceiling, graffiti of the Eye of Horus that pre-dated the exhibition oddly contributed to the compounding of ritualistic symbols that have accumulated in the hall.

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, To Have a Shell is to Surrender, 2026. Halle am Berghain Courtesy of HOUSE in partnership with Autotelic Foundation. Photo: Noshe

There is an inherent futility in worshipping an ancient sun god for any kind of wish-fulfillment. But ritualistic production and devotion have persisted through millennia and in our lives today, be it in our fascination with our illuminated, hand-held screens, or our commitment to attend public dance events that encourage abandon—such as the one that took place the weekend of Roushan and Stankievech’s opening, several metres and a few layers of concrete away. Objects scattered throughout the exhibition space included replicas of Bronze Age bells and reliefs, objects of ritual and religious significance that adorned the space and further contextualised the spiritual dimension of the installation, although none so potent as the ever-burning incense.

“We are all subject to digital change in society, economy, politics,” Nadim Samman, the Autotelic Foundation’s director, says. “These changes are massive, they are abrupt and they are intense. Making sense of it needs to come from below. Artists offer important ways to understand what this change means. Without artists, we are just stuck with the narratives and the images supplied by the companies that make the technology. Digital change impacts culture, and artists can help us understand what that impact actually feels like, and how we can think it through."

Ala Roushan and Charles Stankievech, The Most Naked Thing in Existence, 2026. Halle am Berghain Courtesy of HOUSE in partnership with Autotelic Foundation. Photo: Noshe

Since we have no recordings of PII, it is hard to refute that AI contributed a vital component of the installation. The exhibition’s thoughtful and archaeological approach to incorporating technologies that help us to speculate on the past was a welcome counterpoint to many digital art exhibitions in recent memory.

Roushan and Stankievech’s ambitious undertaking stands as one of the highlights of what has been a busy year for digital and AI-generated artistic presentations. The rather romantic gesture of leveraging AI to take us back several thousand years, to a time of fascination with nature and solar worship, and the decision to do so within the Brutalist context of a power-station-cum-nightclub, cleverly brought together many disparate threads.

“Studio Berlin” offers a way to circumvent the toughest door policy in the world and see works by Olafur Eliasson, Wolfgang Tillmans and Anne Imhof

The opening of Digital Art Fair in Hong Kong is a moment to take stock of how technological developments have changed the nature of digital art

Both AI-generated art and digital art have had a tough time lately, but the 61st Venice Biennale offers the digitally-minded some work to feel good about

Holiday Gift Guide 2026

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With the holiday gift-giving season upon us, it’s time to think about the gaming fans in your life and find the ideal present to brighten their day. And if you’re the gamer in the family, we always encourage you to circle relevant items in the coming pages and leave your magazine open in hard-to-miss locations around the house. We’ve scoured dozens of tech products, tabletop games, collectibles, and more to select and highlight some of the coolest items we found this year.

We recommended the Board shortly after its launch, but with its expanded game library and lower price than first-adopter rates, this holiday season is an ideal time to make the leap. This family-friendly platform features original games that sit comfortably between the digital and physical divide. The horizontally placed screen shows the backdrop of one of the several included games, or the sold-separately add-on games that have popped in since launch. Each game has its own real physical play pieces that interact with the action onscreen, often in innovative and charming ways. Think of it as the best of both worlds between board games and video games, and you’ll get what they’re shooting for here. $399

Few additions to the latest Dungeons & Dragons rules pack as much punch and value as this new expansion, focusing on ways to develop further and evolve the use of magic across your tabletop gaming sessions. New subclasses, spells, creatures, magical items, and factions add thoughtful, intriguing options for both players looking to play someone new and DMs hunting for new story hooks and challenges. $50

If you’re as excited as we are about this month’s celebration of the Tomb Raider legacy (see p. 40), this centerpiece statue is an ideal fit for your collection. Standing roughly nine inches in height, Lara is poised for exploration in her original classic garb. The sculpt strikes a perfect balance between crisp detail and stylistic presentation, in line with that early version of the character; the metallic blue motorcycle paint is especially striking. $250

For players longing for the glory days of stand-up arcade games, it’s hard to beat Arcade 1Up’s approach. The team’s sturdy cabinets look and sound great as a marquee feature of your game room. The new Outrun machine features much of the original display art, a bright 19-inch screen, and responsive gas and brake pedals as you steer around those tight corners. Sega has packed in OutRun, Turbo OutRun, OutRunners, and Power Drift. If old-school racing isn’t your thing, look for cabinets that run the gamut from Pac-Man to Joust to Claw Machines. $629.99

The long-running Nendoroid figures continue to be an attractive but still affordable choice for building collections across numerous fan-favorite series. There are somewhere around 2000 characters at this point! New entries like the Genshin Impact collection make a strong case for the line. The look is chibi, the quality is excellent, poseability is high, and customizable face and accessory parts let you change up your display over time. $46

We’ve previously sung the praises of Libernovo’s versatile approach to an ergonomic gaming chair, which includes a lumbar stretch functionality, a steady recline option for comfortable pushback without sudden shifts, and dynamically adjusting parts as you move through your day. The Maxis line does all that, but addresses one of the fundamental problems for at least some users with the original – it wasn’t big enough. With features like a wider base and longer headrest reach, the Maxis aims to provide the same comfort to the players who are tall or wide enough to need the extra breathing room. $1119

Privacy around smart glasses has been a major point of contention as wearable tech continues to expand. Even G2s have a clear answer to that issue by consciously leaving out a camera. Instead, these glasses focus on productivity and specific tasks, like real-time translation into 35 languages, in-lens teleprompters for presentations, and calendar notifications – all presented in a readable display that is largely invisible from the outside. $600

If you’re the player on your team who likes to have easy access to soundboards and real-time voice changing, the Key Pocket could be just the ticket. This small device straps onto your phone for mobile play, but it’s also compatible with consoles, Discord, Twitch, or just about any place you want to tweak audio on the fly. A free tier gives you access to some voice-changing and sounds, while a separate subscription opens up hundreds of thousands of additional options. But be warned: we can’t help you after your friends abandon you for that baby voice you won’t stop using. $99

The question of the best bang for your buck in modern consoles has become extraordinarily complicated, especially as tech prices have soared in recent years. There’s no right answer, but Sony’s high-end machine walks the line of offering extremely high-tier performance without escalating to even higher-priced gaming PCs. Higher and more consistent frame rates than on standard PS5, advanced ray tracing, and an increasing number of games that feature enhanced PSSR image options all add up to a better gaming experience, if you’re ready to make the significant price jump. $900

If the gamer in your life is looking for a stylish, understated way to express their love of the hobby, Anicorn’s designer watch collaboration with PlayStation could be a standout option. Available in black and a slick silver/white combo, the minimalist timepiece includes the familiar face buttons of Sony’s controller encased in an oil-injection display so they slowly and freely float across the watch as you move. $250

It’s a safe bet you’ve at least seen the popular Shashibo puzzle cubes by this point. We’ve been fans of their brain-teasing, fidgety fun for some time. Battle Shapes takes the core concept – reshaping the box into various distinct magnetically-locked shapes – and extends it into a timed game experience, playable solo or head-to-head. By targeting specific shapes, you significantly improve your speed with the toy, and scannable QR codes on the cards offer clear tutorials for each shape so you can prep for competition. $50

While the vast majority of gamers looking for good personal audio are going to be okay with a larger set of cans over their ears, We know more than a few players for whom that’s a definite no – for comfort, awareness of surrounding sounds, or other reasons. The Arc 5 earbuds aim to solve for that, with an over-ear design that feels extremely comfortable. And because they sit on the ear, you can still hear what’s going on around you. While that limits some aspects of the final sound quality, the inclusion of THX certification, Dolby Atmos optimization, and Dynamic Bass Enhancement helps to ensure audio is still very strong. $230

Across Lego’s many recent themes, Tolkien’s legendarium has offered some stellar sets. The largest yet is the capital of Gondor, with an astonishing 8,278 pieces and a height of roughly two feet. The castle/city build is impressive, featuring tons of interesting angles and turns. The finished product aims to offer the best of both worlds in presentation – a microscale representation of the front façade and a reverse minifigure-scale Hall of Kings. We especially like the inclusion of Denethor’s cherry tomatoes. $650

The original Azul is often cited as a foundational entry in modern board gaming. The “Kids” versions of celebrated titles aren’t always a sure bet, but this one holds up to scrutiny. Azul Kids lowers the complexity threshold but keeps the foundational strategic thinking. Players lay out uniquely shaped tiles into mosaic frameworks in colorful, appealing patterns; the basic concept of the original inspiration is clear, and kids as young as five can grasp and enjoy the fun. $25

Many corners of the collecting world have embraced the fun (and frequent despair) of blind boxes, where you never know exactly who you’ll get in a given series. Funism has a strong track record for quality, offering adorable smaller figures like their latest line of Evangelion collectibles. Each figure stands nearly four inches and features both human characters and mecha. You can buy singles and roll the dice, or purchase larger sets with a no-duplicates guarantee, which can save a lot of heartache if you’re trying to line your whole shelf. $21

If you’re going to spring for a high-end keyboard for PC gaming, it’s important that you like the feel of the keys. The slightly smaller overall 75% size profile of this Blackwidow has no impact on the great feel of typing – a clear highlight for the unit. The customizable, colored backlights are also a big selling point, as is the side command dial and built-in OLED display, which can be tweaked to show PC performance, GIFs, lighting brightness, and more. It’s a strong contender in a crowded field, and justifies its price with both comfort and customizability. $300

Beautiful, vibrant art and a refreshing take on the classic area-control board game genre are the two strongest reasons to take a close look at Wroth. Players control one of five races vying for supremacy across a conflict-ridden peninsula. Factions are asymmetric, so everyone has unique capabilities, and that adds significant replayability. Players roll and draft dice, allowing distinct actions that move the game forward. And move it does; unlike many games of this ilk, Wroth can be learned quickly and played to completion in a little over an hour. $65

Console and computer hardware prices are skyrocketing with no sign of slowing, and perhaps as a result, retro gaming handhelds are gaining popularity. Many quality devices are available, but what sets the Evercade Nexus apart is its officially licensed game cartridges. Imagine the nostalgic joy of a Game Boy, but with a cartridge that includes both Banjo-Kazooie and Tooie. The cartridge boxes even include manuals. For fans of physical media, Evercade Nexus is a great option. $199

This tiny USB-C plug-in (with included USB-A adapter) solves a bunch of audio challenges in one uniform device. It offers high-resolution Bluetooth transmission on iPhone, high-bitrate audio transmission to compatible LDAC headphones, enables high-quality Bluetooth output from your PC, and even low-latency Bluetooth audio on PS5 and Switch. In short, it takes a huge number of products with a USB port and ensures a dynamic Bluetooth audio connection with relative ease. $99

The original MV6 is a familiar sight on streams thanks to its clear voice reproduction and overall ease of use. Shure has built on that success with the Gen 2, offering an excellent standalone mic that doesn’t break the bank. Available in black or white, the Gen 2 features a handy auto-level mode that keeps volume even as you adjust your own amplitude, an adjustable denoiser for your space, a longer cable for out-of-the-box convenience, and three presets for plug-and-play use. In short, it’s an excellent choice if you want great performance without needing an audio engineering degree. $169

XR Glasses are still fighting for awareness, but once you try them, it’s easy to see why the concept is on the rise. Viture’s Pro 2 makes an impressive case for adoption, with a competitive price point, comfortable wear, and a giant 146-inch screen that floats in front of your face for gaming or work on your PC or phone. The display is crisp, bright, and impressive, even as the glasses are light and easy to use. With a mobile dock mini (sold separately), you can even snap onto a Switch 2 with ease and start playing instantly. $299

Role-playing gamers have several excellent universal systems to pick between when considering a story or campaign idea that might fall outside traditional genre staples. Among many good options, Cypher stands out as one of the very best. The system uses a rules-light, narrative-focused approach that prioritizes player-driven storytelling across everything from hard sci-fi to superheroes to intrigue- driven espionage. This is the corebook that’s needed to run and play the full system; additional books only add options. $50

As gamers have continually migrated toward personal audio that can rock amazing sound without disturbing the whole house, high-end premium headphones have become a dangerously competitive space. Turtle Beach meets that challenge head-on, with a robust option that sounds beautiful in use. The Stealth Pro II boasts the slick ability to wirelessly switch between different devices, which is awesome if you’re one of those players who regularly jumps between platforms to play your favorite titles. Like the whole category, it’s a lot to spend on a headset, but Dolby Atmos, active noise cancellation, and 60mm dual drivers all add up to a powerful argument. $300

With an increasing number of top-tier console and PC games like Resident Evil Village, No Man’s Sky, and Dead Cells now available on iOS, not to mention the consistent quality of Apple Arcade, Apple phones remain the best mobile platform for gaming. The 18 Pro Max phone has implemented a number of tweaks that strengthen the platform, including higher memory, much faster graphics processing, impressive HDR performance, and notably improved battery life, all adding up to a gaming experience that is working hard to justify your playtime when away from home. Starting at $1299

SteelSeries is best known for its strong audio line-up, but its push into premium-tier controllers exhibits a similar flair for quality and usability. The new Aeon Pro has a lot to recommend it as your go-to Xbox, PC, or mobile choice. The two included batteries (one of which can always be charging in the dock) ensure essentially unlimited playtime. The full-pull or hair triggers and buttons are clicky and satisfying, and the Hall Effect thumbsticks are built to minimize any drift. Textured handgrips and overall heft are ideal. And an onboard OLED lets you adjust profiles and button assignments, switch between platforms, and check battery at a glance. $260

Valve’s pricey VR headset isn’t a revolution in the medium, but the company has been invested in the technology since its inception. As a result, the Steam Frame is a very good, very comfortable headset. Like its competition, the Quest, its best use is playing games locally on the headset, and Steam’s library is vast and interesting. Wireless connected play to a powerful PC is also well done, but currently the included Wireless Adapter is not fully compatible with the Steam Machine – a baffling limitation we were shocked to confirm. VR is an expensive sub-hobby within video games, but Steam Frame is among the best ways to explore and enjoy it. $1059

Many of the coolest “gaming lights” focus on the flexibility and fun of bright RGB customization. While we’re fans of that aesthetic, it leaves others out in the cold who might want something a bit more natural. The Elements hex panels feature a natural wood-inspired panel, customizable “scenes” like a campfire glow, and can react to both music and touch, with adjustable ambient white light to keep the vibe relaxed. $200

Kyle Hilliard contributed to this article.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: car most similar to a 2014 Chevy Impala--minimal digital gadgetry in the cockpit

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Much as I like the idea of an Avalon, there do not appear to be any near me with acceptable mileage.

She learned this week that her Impala will not pass inspection due to a rusted frame. She has a week to buy a new car, so she can't dawdle and is probably limited to what she can find locally.

She is dead set on a sedan and has not interest in any SUV. I think that is sensible.

Went out looking today. Two she liked:

1) 2020 Toyota Camry Hybrid, 26K miles, CPO, asking price around $24,500 (I can't remember exactly). At a Toyota dealer. She did not set out to look for a hybrid, but neither would she object. The higher MPG would be nice, but I suppose the hybrid system adds some complexity. Her trusted mechanics are at a very conveniently-located Chevy dealer. They could do the basics but it's a safe assumption they would not work on the Toyota hybrid system.

2) 2022 Honda Accord, 52K miles, asking price around $23,000. In my estimation, the Accord is the closest match to Impala size/shape/driving feel that we are likely to find nearby and in time. The instrument panel display was clean and uncluttered. Even this would be a fuel-economy improvement over her 2014 Impala. It is probably easily serviceable just about anywhere---your basic car.

Opinions?
Honestly you can get a bare bones brand new 2027 Toyota Camry for sub $25K. So I'd rather do that than buy a 5-7 year old car for the same price...
I haven't been shopping for cars lately, but it might still be the case that for the reliable toyota/honda options, there's minimal discount for buying used...
I would rather buy a 2027 for sub-$25k too but you might want to say where you're finding a price on a Camry so far below MSRP, much less an OTD price.
